### 步骤

1. **读取所有正文 tex 文件**：
   - `abstract.tex`
   - `introduction.tex`
   - `experiments.tex`
   - `conclusion.tex`（包含 Discussion + Conclusion + Limitations）

2. **提取每个数字 claim**：逐个提取所有定量声明（F1 值、百分比、倍数、pp 差值等）

3. **在 Table/Figure 中溯源**：对每个数字，找到对应的 Table 或 Figure 数据。Table 定义在 `experiments.tex` 和 `appendix.tex` 中。

4. **特别核对以下 abstract 数字**：
   - "F1=0.97--1.0" — 应对应 Table 2 或 Table 3 的 9-class OOD 结果
   - "0.26--0.31" — vanilla baseline OOD 结果
   - "F1=0.504" — ActorAttack mixed-LLM 结果（Table 5）
   - "0.837 vs 0.596" — ToxicChat MLM vs classification（Table 6）
   - "58--68%" — FPR before adaptation
   - "0--1.5%" — FPR after adaptation
   - "24.3×" — speedup vs LLM
   - "43×" — parameter ratio（"43× fewer parameters"）
   - "1.8%" — FPR 数字

5. **检查四舍五入一致性**：同一个数字在不同位置是否用了一致的精度（如 0.997 vs 1.0 vs 99.7%）

6. **检查 introduction.tex 的 surprise value claims**：
   - "over 60 percentage points" — 核对具体数字
   - "scrambled labels match correct labels" — 核对 TOST 或 Table 3 数据
   - "hierarchy reverses entirely on human-authored data" — 核对 Table 6

7. **检查 conclusion.tex (Discussion + Conclusion) 的所有数字**：
   - "F1=0.541±0.207" — topic OOD
   - "within 3.9 pp" — scrambled vs correct
   - "F1≥0.97" — MLM detection
   - "participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs 25--32"
   - "+18--42 pp" — ToxicChat improvement
   - "0.818" — mixed-LLM 9-class
   - "AA=1.000 vs 9-class 0.946" — RoBERTa-large
   - "68.1%" — WildChat FPR
   - "Δ≤1 pp" — mean pooling vs BiGRU
   - "N=155" — length-matched
   - "Δ≤2.5 pp" — length-matched change
   - "-57.9 pp" — zero-padding collapse
   - Conclusion: "F1=0.97--1.0 vs vanilla 0.26--0.31"、"F1=0.504"、"0.837 vs 0.596"、"58--68%"、"0--1.5%"
   - Limitations: "12--14 pp"、"κ=0.78"、"184M vs 355M"、"125M--355M"、"n≤49"

## 1. abstract.tex

```latex
% abstract.tex
\begin{abstract}
Multi-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns. Existing defenses fail to generalize: Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks zero-shot in our evaluation. Detecting such attacks benefits substantially from domain adaptation at base model scale, but the form of adaptation matters. We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at base scale (125M--184M parameters) on synthetic multi-turn data decompose contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, and label granularity across three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families. On synthetic data, classification-based adaptation recovers OOD detection (F1\,=\,0.97--1.0 vs.\ vanilla 0.26--0.31; attack-side OOD, IID benign upper bound) while unsupervised pretraining collapses; under mixed-LLM training, template attacks remain detectable but strategy-diverse attacks degrade to chance level (F1\,=\,0.504). On human-authored single-turn data (ToxicChat), this hierarchy reverses: masked language modeling outperforms 9-class classification (F1 0.837 vs.\ 0.596). Classification adaptation's advantage is thus condition-specific rather than universal. Orthogonal to attack-side generalization, few-shot benign adaptation reduces false-positive rates from 58--68\% to 0--1.5\%, with 24.3$\times$ inference speedup (43$\times$ fewer parameters) over LLM-as-judge baselines.
\end{abstract}
```

## 2. introduction.tex

```latex
% introduction.tex

\section{Introduction}

Multi-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.

Existing defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}

Pretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 (across prefix lengths) with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization at base model scale benefits substantially from domain adaptation.

Our pipeline, \textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection)\footnote{We use `disentangled' to describe our experimental methodology of isolating individual factors through controlled ablation, distinct from disentangled representation learning ($\beta$-VAE, InfoGAN).}, separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}
\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}
\label{fig:architecture}
\end{figure}

Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\,=\,0.289 $\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation.

While domain-adaptive pretraining generally improves downstream NLP tasks~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}, three findings in our safety-detection setting are unexpected: (i)~classification DAPT outperforms MLM by over 60 percentage points on OOD detection, despite both using identical domain data---the classification objective itself, not just domain exposure, drives the OOD benefit; (ii)~scrambled labels match correct labels for template-attack detection, suggesting the classification objective's benefit is independent of label semantics; and (iii)~this hierarchy reverses entirely on human-authored data, revealing condition-specific rather than universal advantages of supervised adaptation.

Our contributions:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]
  \item \textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes (attack-side and benign-side), the first, to our knowledge, such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.26--0.31 $\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training; on the benign side, 10--25 conversations reduce FPR from 58--68\% to 0--1.5\% (WildChat, OASST2).

  \item \textbf{Partition sufficiency with an observed difficulty gradient (at base model scale).} Domain-relevant classification objectives, including scrambled labels, suffice for cross-attack generalization on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $|d| \leq 0.14$ on DD, $d \approx 0$ on FITD at $n$\,=\,10), while attacks employing more varied conversational strategies (ActorAttack) show a moderate label effect ($d$\,=\,0.44). This is consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework: domain-relevant partitioning appears necessary (topic labels fail) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12. A probe over these dimensions confirms sufficiency; removing them degrades DD by 17\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).

  \item \textbf{Two-step adaptation protocol.} Continued pretraining achieves 24.3$\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge (43$\times$ fewer parameters; Appendix~\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds without target-domain attack data.
\end{itemize}
```

---

## 3. experiments.tex

```latex
% experiments.tex

\section{Experiments}

\paragraph{Terminology.}
\emph{Cross-attack OOD}: evaluation on attack families unseen during training; benign distribution remains IID throughout (OOD reflects attack-side shift only). $K$: prefix length (number of conversation turns retained). \emph{DAPT}: all Stage-1 adaptations; we extend \citet{gururangan2020dontstop} to supervised variants. \emph{Evidence hierarchy} (on synthetic data): vanilla $<$ MLM $<$ classification (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).

\subsection{Setup}

\paragraph{Data.}
All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} (non-discriminative; Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}) + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
No overlap exists in attack templates across families; benign is IID (same generation pipeline).
A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.

\paragraph{Models.}
Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
Our vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU configuration is architecturally equivalent to DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} and differs only in encoder choice (DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ BERT); it serves as our primary baseline.
For cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
We also compare TF-IDF LR, LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}), and Llama Guard~3~8B~\citep{metallamaguard3_2024} (zero-shot; evaluates last-response safety given conversation context), which detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks (5/278 pooled recall; Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).

\paragraph{Metrics.}
F1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}).
OOD results report 3-seed mean$\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants.
For key pairwise comparisons, we report BCa bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals (10,000 resamples over individual predictions pooled across 3 seeds; this assumes cross-seed independence and is exploratory) and paired permutation tests (10,000 permutations). Seed-level F1 values (Table~\ref{tab:seed_level}, Appendix~\ref{app:seed_level}) confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed.
All standard deviations are computed over 3 random seeds using the population formula ($n$ divisor); Bessel-corrected estimates ($n{-}1$ divisor) are $\approx$22\% larger but do not alter any significance conclusions.

\paragraph{Training.}
Stage-1: learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\%.
Stage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\small
\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1) at $K$=2 and full conversation length. IID: Crescendo; OOD: Deceptive Delight (DD). 3-seed mean$\pm$std where noted; full per-$K$ in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}
\label{tab:main_results}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}
\toprule
& \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\
\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\
\midrule
9-class & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & .981$\pm$.013 & \textbf{.997$\pm$.005} \\
MLM-only$^{\ddagger}$ & .974 & 1.000 & \textbf{.993$\pm$.009} & .914$\pm$.115 \\
Vanilla & .978$\pm$.016 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 & .312$\pm$.026 \\
TF-IDF LR$^{\S}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\vspace{-2pt}
{\footnotesize $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID reports single seed (42); OOD reports 3-seed. $^{\S}$TF-IDF is deterministic (no std).}
\end{table}

Vanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).
On OOD DD, vanilla drops to F1\,=\,0.312$\pm$0.026 and worsens with more turns (0.628$\to$0.312 at Full), consistent with attack turns occupying benign embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}. MLM recovers to $\geq$0.97 at $K \leq 5$ without labels but is unstable at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115); 9-class maintains 0.997$\pm$0.005.
On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses (0.261) while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026, a 71\,pp gap confirming domain-adapted representations capture beyond-lexical features.
Seed-level results confirm consistent separation (Table~\ref{tab:seed_level}; bootstrap CIs non-overlapping, permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD is non-discriminative (all F1\,$\geq$\,0.990).

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}
\caption{Cross-attack overview (F1 at full conversation length). DisPeD (9-class) maintains near-perfect detection across IID and both OOD families; vanilla collapses on OOD DD. FITD is non-discriminative for all methods.}
\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}
\end{figure}

Prefix-length analysis (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:full_prefix}) confirms 9-class stability across all prefixes (0.972--0.997) vs.\ MLM instability at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115) and vanilla degradation (0.628$\to$0.312).

%----------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}
\label{sec:decomposition}

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\small
\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD DD and ActorAttack (AA) at full conversation length (3-seed mean$\pm$std where available). OOD F1 reflects attack-side generalization only; benign samples are IID from training distribution (benign-side shift evaluated in Section~\ref{sec:benign_shift}). Bold indicates best per column. All variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline. Full per-prefix results in Appendix Tables~\ref{tab:full_prefix} and~\ref{tab:actorattack_full}. Bottom rows: full dual-OOD evaluation with WildChat benign replacing IID benign (Appendix~\ref{app:dual_ood}).}
\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}
\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & AA-Full & DD-$K$=2 \\
\midrule
9-class (full model) & .997$\pm$.005 & .972$\pm$.026 & .981$\pm$.013 \\
Binary (2-class) & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{.990$\pm$.008} & .994$\pm$.009 \\
Scrambled labels & .997$\pm$.005 & .951$\pm$.043 & .981$\pm$.026 \\
MLM-only$^{\ddagger}$ (no labels) & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 & .993$\pm$.009 \\
Mean pooling$^{*}$ & .991$\pm$.013 & .962$\pm$.015 & \textbf{1.000} \\
\midrule
Topic classif.\ (5-class) & .541$\pm$.207 & .625$\pm$.141 & .593$\pm$.166 \\
Wikipedia MLM & .904$\pm$.115 & .686$\pm$.297 & .911$\pm$.073 \\
Vanilla DeBERTa & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 \\
TF-IDF LR$^{\dagger}$ & .961 & .261 & \textbf{1.000} \\
Llama Guard~3$^{\P}$ & .025 & .000 & -- \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Dual-OOD (WildChat benign; Section~\ref{sec:benign_shift})}} \\[2pt]
9-class (no adapt) & .855 & .842 & -- \\
9-class (+10-shot) & .994 & .981 & -- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\vspace{-2pt}
{\footnotesize $^{*}$9-class encoder with mean pooling replacing BiGRU. $^{\dagger}$Deterministic (no std). $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}) is single-seed (42); all OOD values are 3-seed. $^{\P}$Zero-shot; no domain training.}
\end{table}

A clear \emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants on synthetic multi-turn data (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}, Figure~\ref{fig:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length: vanilla $<$ topic $\ll$ MLM $<$ classification-supervised ($\approx$\,1.0 on DD).

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_evidence_hierarchy.pdf}
\caption{Evidence hierarchy across evaluation conditions. Classification-based DAPT outperforms MLM and vanilla on synthetic OOD attacks (DD, AA), but the hierarchy reverses on human-authored single-turn data (ToxicChat), where JB-MLM leads.}
\label{fig:evidence_hierarchy}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary observed factor.}
MLM bridges $\sim$60\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\pm$0.026 $\to$ 0.914$\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.
All three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.997--1.000); McNemar's ($p > 0.13$) and paired permutation tests ($p \geq 0.40$) provide descriptive evidence suggesting equivalence. Extended to $n$\,=\,10 seeds, TOST (margin = 5\,pp) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d$\,=\,$-$0.14; Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) and FITD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \approx 0$). Label quality and granularity are non-factors for template-attack detection (DD/FITD: $|d| \leq 0.14$), but strategy-diverse ActorAttack retains moderate label sensitivity ($d$\,=\,0.44, TOST $p$\,=\,0.183). For template attacks, any domain-relevant classification objective suffices; the objective's structural role---partitioning the representation space---matters more than label semantics. Strategy-diverse attacks mark a difficulty boundary for this sufficiency.
Classification supervision is associated with 3--23$\times$ lower cross-seed variance over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\ 0.115 on DD; 3 seeds; Appendix~\ref{app:ensemble}), though this reverses on human-authored data (Section~\ref{sec:human_data}).

\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}
Wikipedia MLM (0.904$\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\ 0.911).
Perturbation analysis (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.

\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}
Topic classification's poor performance (0.541$\pm$0.207) is consistent with misaligned supervision; the cleaner test isolating domain exposure is Wiki-MLM vs.\ JB-MLM (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).

\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on strategy-diverse attacks.}
ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} preserves the same hierarchy (Figure~\ref{fig:evidence_hierarchy}) with wider gaps: TF-IDF collapses (0.261) while classification variants converge (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\,pp); DAPT provides $\sim$71\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\ref{sec:actorattack_full},~\ref{app:aa_error}).

\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}
Mean pooling matches BiGRU (F1 gap $\leq$ 1\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:temporal}) and turn-order shuffling is negligible ($|\Delta| < 1$\,pp), confirming detection relies on per-turn content rather than sequential patterns.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}
\label{sec:cross_encoder}

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\small
\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. Bold indicates best per encoder group.}
\label{tab:cross_encoder}
\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\[2pt]
Vanilla & .312$\pm$.026 & .258$\pm$.000 \\
MLM & .914$\pm$.115 & .893$\pm$.080 \\
9-class & \textbf{.997$\pm$.005} & \textbf{.972$\pm$.026} \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\[2pt]
Vanilla & .981$\pm$.026 & .466$\pm$.163 \\
MLM & .987$\pm$.018 & .730$\pm$.201 \\
9-class & \textbf{.994$\pm$.005} & \textbf{.939$\pm$.046} \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\[2pt]
Vanilla & .927$\pm$.036 & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} \\
MLM & .884$\pm$.065 & .874$\pm$.014 \\
9-class & \textbf{.987$\pm$.004} & .946$\pm$.057 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}}
\end{table}

To test whether the evidence hierarchy depends on encoder choice, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).
On DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\pm$0.026 (vs.\ DeBERTa 0.312$\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\pm$0.163, a 51.5\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\pm$0.046).

RoBERTa-large (355M) reverses the DAPT advantage on ActorAttack (vanilla 1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946; Table~\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), revealing a difficulty threshold: base-scale encoders require DAPT for all attacks, while sufficient capacity renders it unnecessary. A BERT-base proxy confirms DAPT, not architecture, drives generalization (Appendix~\ref{app:bert_proxy}).

%----------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}

All preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).
Classification-based DAPT is the only variant that substantially outperforms vanilla: 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 (+53\,pp over vanilla 0.291$\pm$0.065), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289$\pm$0.101). On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, detection degrades to chance level (9-class F1\,=\,0.504$\pm$0.107, indistinguishable from majority baseline at $n$\,=\,3).
Mixed-LLM training confirms that classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources where unsupervised pretraining does not (full results in Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}); per-source data reduction (250 vs.\ 350) is an alternative explanation we cannot yet disentangle.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Human Data Validation}
\label{sec:human_data}

Since both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).

\begin{table}[t]
\centering
\small
\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1, 3-seed mean$\pm$std). Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}
\label{tab:human_data}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\
\midrule
JB-MLM & \textbf{.837$\pm$.013} \\
Wiki-MLM & .791$\pm$.093 \\
Binary & .705$\pm$.072 \\
Scrambled & .616$\pm$.056 \\
9-class & .596$\pm$.087 \\
Topic & .496$\pm$.042 \\
\midrule
Vanilla & .413$\pm$.021 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The ordering reverses (Figure~\ref{fig:evidence_hierarchy}): JB-MLM leads (0.837$\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$). All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\,pp, confirming transfer to human-written attacks. ToxicChat is single-turn, so the classification head provides no advantage.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Robustness and Strategy Analysis}
\label{sec:perturbation}

Under adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification variants achieve zero degradation on OOD DD while non-adapted models degrade by $\sim$12\,pp; on ActorAttack, DAPT degrades ($\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4\,pp) but maintains 52\,pp over vanilla (Table~\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}; Appendix~\ref{sec:perturbation_full}). We distinguish \emph{template attacks} (transition rate $<$\,0.1; e.g., Crescendo, DD) from \emph{structurally complex attacks} (rate $>$\,0.3; e.g., ActorAttack). The 9-class taxonomy characterizes known attacks ($\kappa$\,=\,0.78) but degrades on OOD ($\kappa$\,=\,0.126; Appendix~\ref{app:strategy_pred}); detection operates through \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not strategy accuracy.

%----------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Benign Distribution Shift}
\label{sec:benign_shift}

On WildChat benign conversations, 9-class FPR rises to 68.1\%. Few-shot adaptation (Stage-2 BiGRU, encoder frozen, $N$ benign conversations, BCE loss, 5 epochs) resolves this: 10 WildChat conversations reduce FPR to 0\%, 25 OASST2 to 1.0\%; dual-OOD F1 drops 12--14\,pp without adaptation but recovers within 0.3\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:dual_ood}).
```

## 4. conclusion.tex

```latex
% conclusion.tex

\section{Discussion}
\label{sec:discussion}

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig4_svd_subspace.pdf}
\caption{Test representations projected onto top-2 SVD directions. 9-class DAPT produces clear cluster separation absent in vanilla.}
\label{fig:svd_subspace}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Discriminative compression and label equivalence.}
Three observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97).
Classification DAPT compresses features into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\ 25--32; Figure~\ref{fig:svd_subspace}; Appendix~\ref{app:representation}), consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}: domain-relevant partitioning appears necessary (topic DAPT fails) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12. A probe confirms sufficiency; removal degrades DD by 17\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}). The partition sufficiency finding implies that the classification objective's value lies in its structural role---forcing class-conditional feature organization---rather than in encoding persuasion-specific semantics.

\paragraph{Cross-condition transfer and boundaries.}
ToxicChat (+18--42\,pp) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer beyond single-LLM artifacts; domain exposure is universally beneficial (all DAPT variants exceed vanilla by $\geq$40\,pp on ToxicChat) but the ranking reverses across conditions (MLM leads on human data). Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses. Both data scaling (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_validation}) and mixed-LLM training involve distributional heterogeneity that appears to erode classification DAPT's concentrated feature advantage. At larger scales (355M), DAPT can be counterproductive (RoBERTa-large vanilla AA\,=\,1.000 vs.\ 9-class 0.946), and WildChat FPR rises to 68.1\%, suggesting DAPT encodes LLM-specific stylistic features that limit benign-side generalization. Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($\Delta \leq 1$\,pp), indicating detection relies on per-turn content rather than sequential patterns.

\paragraph{Length as a potential confound.}
Length-matched evaluation ($N$=155) shows negligible change (DD/AA $\Delta \leq$\,2.5\,pp); the $-$57.9\,pp ActorAttack collapse under zero-padding (Appendix~\ref{app:length_independence}) reflects signal density dilution rather than length shortcut removal.

\section{Conclusion}

We decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two complementary findings. On synthetic data at base scale, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (F1\,=\,0.97--1.0 vs.\ vanilla 0.26--0.31), though template-attack robustness does not extend to strategy-diverse scenarios under mixed-LLM training (ActorAttack F1\,=\,0.504); on human-authored data, the hierarchy reverses (ToxicChat MLM 0.837 vs.\ classification 0.596)---the advantage is condition-specific and counterproductive at 355M scale. Few-shot benign adaptation reduces FPR from 58--68\% to 0--1.5\% with 10 conversations. The ablation framework provides a reusable template for diagnosing when domain adaptation succeeds and when it fails in safety-critical NLP tasks.

\section{Limitations}
\label{sec:limitations}

\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Synthetic data only.} All findings derive from synthetic conversations (single LLM, Qwen3-8B; 350 training, 76--118 test, 3 seeds). The evidence hierarchy reverses on human single-turn data (ToxicChat; Section~\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists.
\item \textbf{IID benign upper bound.} OOD F1 figures assume IID benign data; dual-OOD evaluation drops 12--14\,pp without adaptation (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). Few-shot benign adaptation recovers performance but is validated on only two platforms ($n \leq 49$).
\item \textbf{Scope.} Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks are addressed; single-turn token-level attacks and non-text modalities are out of scope. Cross-encoder comparisons (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) confound architecture with parameter count (184M vs.\ 355M). The 50-sample annotation verification ($\kappa$\,=\,0.78) limits evaluation of strategy characterization accuracy; however, the scrambled-label experiment demonstrates that label correctness does not affect template-attack detection. Our experiments cover base-scale encoders (125M--355M); DAPT effects may differ at larger scales, where RoBERTa-large already shows counterproductive adaptation (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
\end{itemize}
```

---

Existing defenses fail to generalize: Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks zero-shot in our evaluation. Detecting such attacks benefits substantially from domain adaptation at base model scale, but the form of adaptation matters. We isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at base scale (125M--184M parameters) on synthetic multi-turn data decompose contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, and label granularity across three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families. On synthetic data, classification-based adaptation recovers OOD detection (F1\\,=\\,0.97--1.0 vs.\\ vanilla 0.26--0.31; attack-side OOD, IID benign upper bound) while unsupervised pretraining collapses; under mixed-LLM training, template attacks remain detectable but strategy-diverse attacks degrade to chance level (F1\\,=\\,0.504). On human-authored single-turn data (ToxicChat), this hierarchy reverses: masked language modeling outperforms 9-class classification (F1 0.837 vs.\\ 0.596). Classification adaptation's advantage is thus condition-specific rather than universal. Orthogonal to attack-side generalization, few-shot benign adaptation reduces false-positive rates from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\%, with 24.3$\\times$ inference speedup (43$\\times$ fewer parameters) over LLM-as-judge baselines.\n\\end{abstract}\n```\n\n---\n\n## 2. introduction.tex\n\n```latex\n% introduction.tex\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 (across prefix lengths) with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization at base model scale benefits substantially from domain adaptation.\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection)\\footnote{We use `disentangled' to describe our experimental methodology of isolating individual factors through controlled ablation, distinct from disentangled representation learning ($\\beta$-VAE, InfoGAN).}, separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation.\n\nWhile domain-adaptive pretraining generally improves downstream NLP tasks~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}, three findings in our safety-detection setting are unexpected: (i)~classification DAPT outperforms MLM by over 60 percentage points on OOD detection, despite both using identical domain data---the classification objective itself, not just domain exposure, drives the OOD benefit; (ii)~scrambled labels match correct labels for template-attack detection, suggesting the classification objective's benefit is independent of label semantics; and (iii)~this hierarchy reverses entirely on human-authored data, revealing condition-specific rather than universal advantages of supervised adaptation.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} On synthetic multi-turn data, thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes (attack-side and benign-side), the first, to our knowledge, such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.26--0.31 $\\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training; on the benign side, 10--25 conversations reduce FPR from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\% (WildChat, OASST2).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Partition sufficiency with an observed difficulty gradient (at base model scale).} Domain-relevant classification objectives, including scrambled labels, suffice for cross-attack generalization on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $|d| \\leq 0.14$ on DD, $d \\approx 0$ on FITD at $n$\\,=\\,10), while attacks employing more varied conversational strategies (ActorAttack) show a moderate label effect ($d$\\,=\\,0.44). This is consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework: domain-relevant partitioning appears necessary (topic labels fail) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12. A probe over these dimensions confirms sufficiency; removing them degrades DD by 17\\,pp while FITD is unaffected (partial necessity; Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Two-step adaptation protocol.} Continued pretraining achieves 24.3$\\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge (43$\\times$ fewer parameters; Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds without target-domain attack data.\n\\end{itemize}\n```\n\n---\n\n## 3. experiments.tex\n\n```latex\n% experiments.tex\n\n\\section{Experiments}\n\n\\paragraph{Terminology.}\n\\emph{Cross-attack OOD}: evaluation on attack families unseen during training; benign distribution remains IID throughout (OOD reflects attack-side shift only). $K$: prefix length (number of conversation turns retained). \\emph{DAPT}: all Stage-1 adaptations; we extend \\citet{gururangan2020dontstop} to supervised variants. \\emph{Evidence hierarchy} (on synthetic data): vanilla $<$ MLM $<$ classification (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Data.}\nAll synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\\citep{qwen3_2025}.\nThe IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).\nStage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; all OOD values are 3-seed. $^{\\P}$Zero-shot; no domain training.}\n\\end{table}\n\nA clear \\emph{evidence hierarchy} emerges across seven variants on synthetic multi-turn data (Table~\\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}, Figure~\\ref{fig:evidence_hierarchy}), ordered by OOD detection at full conversation length: vanilla $<$ topic $\\ll$ MLM $<$ classification-supervised ($\\approx$\\,1.0 on DD).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_evidence_hierarchy.pdf}\n\\caption{Evidence hierarchy across evaluation conditions. Classification-based DAPT outperforms MLM and vanilla on synthetic OOD attacks (DD, AA), but the hierarchy reverses on human-authored single-turn data (ToxicChat), where JB-MLM leads.}\n\\label{fig:evidence_hierarchy}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Domain exposure is the primary observed factor.}\nMLM bridges $\\sim$60\\,pp from vanilla (0.312$\\pm$0.026 $\\to$ 0.914$\\pm$0.115) without labels; this supports the DAPT interpretation~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 9-class 0.972) confirms that domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.\nAll three classification-supervised variants converge to equivalent detection (F1 = 0.997--1.000); McNemar's ($p > 0.13$) and paired permutation tests ($p \\geq 0.40$) provide descriptive evidence suggesting equivalence. Extended to $n$\\,=\\,10 seeds, TOST (margin = 5\\,pp) confirms equivalence on DD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d$\\,=\\,$-$0.14; Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) and FITD ($p < 10^{-8}$, $d \\approx 0$). Label quality and granularity are non-factors for template-attack detection (DD/FITD: $|d| \\leq 0.14$), but strategy-diverse ActorAttack retains moderate label sensitivity ($d$\\,=\\,0.44, TOST $p$\\,=\\,0.183). For template attacks, any domain-relevant classification objective suffices; the objective's structural role---partitioning the representation space---matters more than label semantics. Strategy-diverse attacks mark a difficulty boundary for this sufficiency.\nClassification supervision is associated with 3--23$\\times$ lower cross-seed variance over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\\ 0.115 on DD; 3 seeds; Appendix~\\ref{app:ensemble}), though this reverses on human-authored data (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\n\\paragraph{Domain specificity is marginal at full length but meaningful for early detection.}\nWikipedia MLM (0.904$\\pm$0.115) nearly matches jailbreak-domain MLM at full length (1\\,pp gap), though jailbreak-domain text provides an 8\\,pp advantage at early prefixes ($K$=2: 0.993 vs.\\ 0.911).\nPerturbation analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}) reveals a sharper domain specificity effect: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\\sim$12\\,pp under adversarial paraphrasing while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}\nTopic classification's poor performance (0.541$\\pm$0.207) is consistent with misaligned supervision; the cleaner test isolating domain exposure is Wiki-MLM vs.\\ JB-MLM (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence hierarchy on strategy-diverse attacks.}\nActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} preserves the same hierarchy (Figure~\\ref{fig:evidence_hierarchy}) with wider gaps: TF-IDF collapses (0.261) while classification variants converge (0.951--0.990, max gap 3.9\\,pp); DAPT provides $\\sim$71\\,pp over vanilla (Appendix~\\ref{sec:actorattack_full},~\\ref{app:aa_error}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal modeling.}\nMean pooling matches BiGRU (F1 gap $\\leq$ 1\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:temporal}) and turn-order shuffling is negligible ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp), confirming detection relies on per-turn content rather than sequential patterns.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Cross-Encoder Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross_encoder}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-encoder comparison on OOD Full (3-seed mean$\\pm$std). DD = Deceptive Delight, AA = ActorAttack. Bold indicates best per encoder group.}\n\\label{tab:cross_encoder}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & DD & AA \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{DeBERTa-v3-base (184M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .312$\\pm$.026 & .258$\\pm$.000 \\\\\nMLM & .914$\\pm$.115 & .893$\\pm$.080 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.997$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.972$\\pm$.026} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-base (125M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .981$\\pm$.026 & .466$\\pm$.163 \\\\\nMLM & .987$\\pm$.018 & .730$\\pm$.201 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.994$\\pm$.005} & \\textbf{.939$\\pm$.046} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RoBERTa-large (355M)}} \\\\[2pt]\nVanilla & .927$\\pm$.036 & \\textbf{1.000$\\pm$.000} \\\\\nMLM & .884$\\pm$.065 & .874$\\pm$.014 \\\\\n9-class & \\textbf{.987$\\pm$.004} & .946$\\pm$.057 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nTo test whether the evidence hierarchy depends on encoder choice, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class on RoBERTa-base~\\citep{liu2019roberta} (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_encoder}).\nOn DD, RoBERTa-base vanilla already achieves 0.981$\\pm$0.026 (vs.\\ DeBERTa 0.312$\\pm$0.026), so DAPT adds only 1.3\\,pp. ActorAttack exposes this as DD's simplicity: RoBERTa-base vanilla collapses to 0.466$\\pm$0.163, a 51.5\\,pp drop, while domain-adapted variants recover (9-class: 0.939$\\pm$0.046).\n\nRoBERTa-large (355M) reverses the DAPT advantage on ActorAttack (vanilla 1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946; Table~\\ref{tab:roberta_large_lr}), revealing a difficulty threshold: base-scale encoders require DAPT for all attacks, while sufficient capacity renders it unnecessary. A BERT-base proxy confirms DAPT, not architecture, drives generalization (Appendix~\\ref{app:bert_proxy}).\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Mixed-LLM Training}\n\nAll preceding experiments use single-source (Qwen3-8B) data. To test whether the evidence hierarchy transfers when training data mixes LLM sources, we train on 250 Qwen + 250 Llama-3.1-8B conversations (Table~\\ref{tab:mixed_llm}).\nClassification-based DAPT is the only variant that substantially outperforms vanilla: 9-class achieves DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp over vanilla 0.291$\\pm$0.065), while MLM drops to vanilla level (0.289$\\pm$0.101). On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, detection degrades to chance level (9-class F1\\,=\\,0.504$\\pm$0.107, indistinguishable from majority baseline at $n$\\,=\\,3).\nMixed-LLM training confirms that classification supervision generalizes across LLM sources where unsupervised pretraining does not (full results in Appendix~\\ref{app:mixed_llm}); per-source data reduction (250 vs.\\ 350) is an alternative explanation we cannot yet disentangle.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Human Data Validation}\n\\label{sec:human_data}\n\nSince both training and OOD evaluation data are Qwen3-8B-generated, we validate on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 human-written jailbreak attempts + 204 benign conversations from real LMSYS interactions).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Human data validation on ToxicChat~\\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (macro F1, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). Bold indicates best. Single-turn data from real LMSYS user interactions, independent of Qwen3-8B.}\n\\label{tab:human_data}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & Macro F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nJB-MLM & \\textbf{.837$\\pm$.013} \\\\\nWiki-MLM & .791$\\pm$.093 \\\\\nBinary & .705$\\pm$.072 \\\\\nScrambled & .616$\\pm$.056 \\\\\n9-class & .596$\\pm$.087 \\\\\nTopic & .496$\\pm$.042 \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & .413$\\pm$.021 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe ordering reverses (Figure~\\ref{fig:evidence_hierarchy}): JB-MLM leads (0.837$\\pm$0.013) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596$\\pm$0.087; $p < 0.001$). All DAPT variants exceed vanilla by 18--42\\,pp, confirming transfer to human-written attacks. ToxicChat is single-turn, so the classification head provides no advantage.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Robustness and Strategy Analysis}\n\\label{sec:perturbation}\n\nUnder adversarial paraphrasing (BERTScore 0.92), classification variants achieve zero degradation on OOD DD while non-adapted models degrade by $\\sim$12\\,pp; on ActorAttack, DAPT degrades ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,$-$20.4\\,pp) but maintains 52\\,pp over vanilla (Table~\\ref{tab:ood_perturbation}; Appendix~\\ref{sec:perturbation_full}). We distinguish \\emph{template attacks} (transition rate $<$\\,0.1; e.g., Crescendo, DD) from \\emph{structurally complex attacks} (rate $>$\\,0.3; e.g., ActorAttack). The 9-class taxonomy characterizes known attacks ($\\kappa$\\,=\\,0.78) but degrades on OOD ($\\kappa$\\,=\\,0.126; Appendix~\\ref{app:strategy_pred}); detection operates through \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings, not strategy accuracy.\n\n%----------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Benign Distribution Shift}\n\\label{sec:benign_shift}\n\nOn WildChat benign conversations, 9-class FPR rises to 68.1\\%. Few-shot adaptation (Stage-2 BiGRU, encoder frozen, $N$ benign conversations, BCE loss, 5 epochs) resolves this: 10 WildChat conversations reduce FPR to 0\\%, 25 OASST2 to 1.0\\%; dual-OOD F1 drops 12--14\\,pp without adaptation but recovers within 0.3\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:dual_ood}).\n```\n\n---\n\n## 4. conclusion.tex\n\n```latex\n% conclusion.tex\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig4_svd_subspace.pdf}\n\\caption{Test representations projected onto top-2 SVD directions. 9-class DAPT produces clear cluster separation absent in vanilla.}\n\\label{fig:svd_subspace}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Discriminative compression and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\nClassification DAPT compresses features into few dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\\ 25--32; Figure~\\ref{fig:svd_subspace}; Appendix~\\ref{app:representation}), consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}: domain-relevant partitioning appears necessary (topic DAPT fails) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12. A probe confirms sufficiency; removal degrades DD by 17\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}). The partition sufficiency finding implies that the classification objective's value lies in its structural role---forcing class-conditional feature organization---rather than in encoding persuasion-specific semantics.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-condition transfer and boundaries.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer beyond single-LLM artifacts; domain exposure is universally beneficial (all DAPT variants exceed vanilla by $\\geq$40\\,pp on ToxicChat) but the ranking reverses across conditions (MLM leads on human data). Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains 0.818 while MLM collapses. Both data scaling (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) and mixed-LLM training involve distributional heterogeneity that appears to erode classification DAPT's concentrated feature advantage. At larger scales (355M), DAPT can be counterproductive (RoBERTa-large vanilla AA\\,=\\,1.000 vs.\\ 9-class 0.946), and WildChat FPR rises to 68.1\\%, suggesting DAPT encodes LLM-specific stylistic features that limit benign-side generalization. Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($\\Delta \\leq 1$\\,pp), indicating detection relies on per-turn content rather than sequential patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Length as a potential confound.}\nLength-matched evaluation ($N$=155) shows negligible change (DD/AA $\\Delta \\leq$\\,2.5\\,pp); the $-$57.9\\,pp ActorAttack collapse under zero-padding (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}) reflects signal density dilution rather than length shortcut removal.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe decompose multi-turn jailbreak generalization into two complementary findings. On synthetic data at base scale, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (F1\\,=\\,0.97--1.0 vs.\\ vanilla 0.26--0.31), though template-attack robustness does not extend to strategy-diverse scenarios under mixed-LLM training (ActorAttack F1\\,=\\,0.504); on human-authored data, the hierarchy reverses (ToxicChat MLM 0.837 vs.\\ classification 0.596)---the advantage is condition-specific and counterproductive at 355M scale. Few-shot benign adaptation reduces FPR from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\% with 10 conversations. The ablation framework provides a reusable template for diagnosing when domain adaptation succeeds and when it fails in safety-critical NLP tasks.\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Synthetic data only.} All findings derive from synthetic conversations (single LLM, Qwen3-8B; 350 training, 76--118 test, 3 seeds). The evidence hierarchy reverses on human single-turn data (ToxicChat; Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists.\n\\item \\textbf{IID benign upper bound.} OOD F1 figures assume IID benign data; dual-OOD evaluation drops 12--14\\,pp without adaptation (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). Few-shot benign adaptation recovers performance but is validated on only two platforms ($n \\leq 49$).\n\\item \\textbf{Scope.} Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks are addressed; single-turn token-level attacks and non-text modalities are out of scope. Cross-encoder comparisons (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}) confound architecture with parameter count (184M vs.\\ 355M). The 50-sample annotation verification ($\\kappa$\\,=\\,0.78) limits evaluation of strategy characterization accuracy; however, the scrambled-label experiment demonstrates that label correctness does not affect template-attack detection. Our experiments cover base-scale encoders (125M--355M); DAPT effects may differ at larger scales, where RoBERTa-large already shows counterproductive adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\\end{itemize}\n```\n\n---\n\n## 5. appendix.tex\n\nThis file is extremely large (949 lines) with comprehensive supplementary material. Here are the key sections:\n\n- **LLM-as-Judge Baseline** (lines 5-36): Performance comparison of GPT-5.5 and Qwen3-8B for jailbreak detection\n- **Full Cross-Attack Results by Prefix** (lines 37-69): F1 scores at all prefix lengths\n- **ActorAttack Full Results** (lines 71-107): Detailed results with per-prefix analysis\n- **Perturbation Robustness** (lines 108-164): Adversarial paraphrasing experiments\n- **Strategy Trajectory Analysis** (lines 165-179): Heatmaps of persuasion strategy transitions\n- **CKA Heatmaps** (lines 180-189): Representation similarity analysis\n- **Embedding Space Visualization** (lines 190-201): t-SNE visualizations\n- **Cross-LLM Validation** (lines 202-244): Testing generalization across LLM sources\n- **Mixed-LLM Training Results** (lines 245-265): Results with mixed Qwen/Llama data\n- **Dataset Scale Validation** (lines 266-288): Performance at 1,000 conversation scale\n- **RoBERTa-large Learning Rate Control** (lines 289-308): Hyperparameter sensitivity\n- **TOST Equivalence Sensitivity Analysis** (lines 309-328): Statistical equivalence tests\n- **Partition Robustness** (lines 329-389): Random partition experiments and topic failure analysis\n- **Reproducibility Details** (lines 390-410): Complete training hyperparameters and settings\n- **End-to-End Fine-Tuning Upper Bound** (lines 411-432): Frozen vs. E2E comparison\n- **Real-World False Positive Rate** (lines 433-482): WildChat and OASST2 FPR experiments\n- **BERT-base Proxy Baseline** (lines 506-526): Architecture comparison\n- **Confidence Calibration** (lines 527-550): ECE analysis\n- **Frozen Probing Verification** (lines 551-576): Frozen probing validation\n- **Single-Turn Truncation Analysis** (lines 577-605): Multi-turn vs. single-turn comparison\n- Aggregation Comparison** (lines 704-726): BiGRU vs. mean pooling\n- **Length Stratification Analysis** (lines 727-796): Conversation length breakdown\n- **Inference Efficiency** (lines 797-819): Speed 71	\section{ActorAttack Full Results}

73

100	
101	Table~\ref{tab:actorattack_full} reveals three distinct prefix-length patterns. The 9-class model improves monotonically from $K$=1 to Full (+20\,pp), consistent with cumulative evidence accumulation across turns. Vanilla DeBERTa \emph{decreases} with more turns (0.337 at $K$=1 to 0.258 at Full), suggesting that additional attack turns push representations further into benign embedding regions. MLM peaks at $K$=3 (0.945) then declines to 0.893 at Full, indicating that unsupervised domain exposure captures early-turn patterns but is unstable under extended sequences.
102	
103	\subsection{Error Analysis}
104	\label{app:aa_error}
105	
106	Per-sample majority vote across 3 seeds yields 98.3\% accuracy on ActorAttack OOD (2 false negatives, 0 false positives). No sample is misclassified by all three seeds; all 7 seed-specific errors are false negatives concentrated in shorter conversations (3--6 turns). On these 7 error samples, vanilla achieves 0/3 correct votes per sample, while 9-class majority vote correctly classifies 5/7, confirming that domain adaptation provides robust per-sample coverage even where individual seeds fail.
107

109	\label{sec:perturbation_full}
110

112	\centering
113	\small
114	\caption{Perturbation behavior under adversarial paraphrasing on IID Crescendo ($n$=76). $\Delta$: F1 degradation (pp). Classification-supervised variants achieve zero degradation at full length; this pattern does not generalize to ActorAttack.}
115	\label{tab:perturbation}
116	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
117	\toprule

121	Scrambled & \textbf{0.0} & $-$5.3 & $-$2.6 & \textbf{0.0} \\
122	Binary & \textbf{0.0} & $-$9.2 & $-$2.6 & \textbf{0.0} \\
123	\midrule

132	\end{table}
133	
134	Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} shows perturbation behavior on IID data.
135	Classification-supervised variants (9-class, binary, scrambled) achieve zero degradation at full conversation length regardless of label correctness.
136	Models without jailbreak-domain exposure (Wikipedia MLM, vanilla) degrade by $\sim$12\,pp.
137	McNemar's test finds no significant pairwise differences among classification-supervised variants (all $p > 0.13$), though with $\leq$7 discordant pairs, statistical power is limited.
138	
139	\begin{table}[t]
140	\centering
141	\small
142	\caption{Perturbation robustness on OOD Deceptive Delight ($n$=118). $\Delta$: F1 change (pp) from clean to paraphrased. 3-seed mean$\pm$std where available.}
143	\label{tab:ood_perturbation}
144	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
145	\toprule
146	\textbf{Variant} & Clean Full & Pert.\ Full & $\Delta$ Full & $\Delta$ $K$=1 \\
147	\midrule
148	9-class & .994$\pm$.005 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & +0.6 & +0.6 \\
149	Binary & 1.000$\pm$.000 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & 0.0 & +0.6 \\
150	Scrambled & .994$\pm$.005 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & +0.6 & $-$0.3 \\
151	\midrule
152	JB MLM & .914$\pm$.115 & .941 & +2.7 & 0.0 \\
153	Wiki MLM$^{*}$ & .904$\pm$.115 & .872 & $-$3.3 & $-$3.2 \\

155	\bottomrule
156	\end{tabular}
157	\vspace{-2pt}
158	{\footnotesize $^{*}$Wiki MLM degradation is seed-driven: seed 42 accounts for $-$9.8\,pp; other seeds show 0.0\,pp change.\\$^{\dagger}$Vanilla $\Delta$ Full = +2.7\,pp reflects floor-effect noise (baseline 0.312, std 0.055), not meaningful improvement.}
159	\end{table}
160	
161	On OOD DD, the IID pattern replicates: classification-supervised variants show zero degradation while McNemar's test confirms 9-class vs.\ vanilla significance ($p < 0.001$, 49--78 discordant pairs).
162	On OOD ActorAttack, domain-adapted variants degrade substantially (9-class $\Delta$\,=\,$-$20.4$\pm$13.5\,pp, MLM $-$35.3\,pp), but perturbation narrows rather than closes the adapted vs.\ non-adapted gap: 9-class perturbed F1 = 0.768 vs.\ vanilla 0.248 (McNemar $p < 0.001$).
163	Domain specificity also manifests: Wikipedia MLM degrades by $\sim$12\,pp while jailbreak-domain MLM degrades by only 2.6\,pp.
164	
165	\section{Strategy Trajectory Analysis}
166	\label{sec:strategy_full}
167	
168	\begin{figure*}[t]
169	\centering
170	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_strategy_heatmap.pdf}
171	\caption{Persuasion strategy transition count matrices for three attack families, based on ground-truth intended strategy labels. Strategy labels validated on $n$=50 samples with a single annotator ($\kappa$=0.78); fine-grained trajectory patterns should be interpreted with this limitation. Crescendo exhibits broad coverage across all strategy categories, while FITD and DD concentrate transitions in smaller subsets. Row $i$, column $j$ counts transitions from strategy $i$ at turn $t$ to strategy $j$ at turn $t+1$.}
172	\label{fig:strategy_heatmap}
173	\end{figure*}
174	
175	Figure~\ref{fig:strategy_heatmap} shows transition count matrices for three attack families.
176	Crescendo conversations exhibit broad transition coverage across all nine strategy categories (1,818 total transitions), with the highest concentration in Role Assignment self-transitions (545); this reflects its gradual escalation design.
177	FITD conversations show a markedly sparser pattern (541 transitions), with Role$\to$Obfuscation (161) as the dominant transition, consistent with FITD's commitment-consistency exploitation~\citep{cialdini2001influence}.
178	DD conversations span 5 active source strategies (565 transitions), with Emotion$\to$Obfuscation (129) as the dominant transition, aligning with DD's design of embedding harmful content within emotionally engaging contexts~\citep{deceptivedelight2024}.
179	
180	\section{Centered CKA Heatmaps}
181	\label{sec:cka_heatmap}
182	
183	\begin{figure}[t]
184	\centering
185	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/cka_heatmap.pdf}
186	\caption{Centered CKA similarity~\citep{kornblith2019cka} between encoder variants on IID and OOD data for DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa. Low off-diagonal values (vanilla vs.\ adapted variants) confirm that continued pretraining substantially reshapes representations; the MLM vs.\ 9-class block shows distinct geometries (CKA $\leq$ 0.2 on RoBERTa OOD).}
187	\label{fig:cka_heatmap}
188	\end{figure}
189	
190	\section{Embedding Space Visualization}
191	\label{app:tsne}
192	
193	Figure~\ref{fig:tsne} visualizes mean-pooled \texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via t-SNE for three representative variants (Vanilla, JB-MLM, 9-Class) on DD OOD and ActorAttack. Vanilla embeddings show dispersed jailbreak clusters with ambiguous decision boundaries, while domain-adapted variants (JB-MLM, 9-Class) produce tighter, more separable clusters. JB-MLM achieves the clearest bimodal separation on both evaluation sets, consistent with its superior detection performance.

199	\label{fig:tsne}

Row $i$, column $j$ counts transitions from strategy $i$ at turn $t$ to strategy $j$ at turn $t+1$.}\n\\label{fig:strategy_heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:strategy_heatmap} shows transition count matrices for three attack families.\nCrescendo conversations exhibit broad transition coverage across all nine strategy categories (1,818 total transitions), with the highest concentration in Role Assignment self-transitions (545); this reflects its gradual escalation design.\nFITD conversations show a markedly sparser pattern (541 transitions), with Role$\\to$Obfuscation (161) as the dominant transition, consistent with FITD's commitment-consistency exploitation~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}.\nDD conversations span 5 active source strategies (565 transitions), with Emotion$\\to$Obfuscation (129) as the dominant transition, aligning with DD's design of embedding harmful content within emotionally engaging contexts~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024}.\n\n\\section{Centered CKA Heatmaps}\n\\label{sec:cka_heatmap}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/cka_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Centered CKA similarity~\\citep{kornblith2019cka} between encoder variants on IID and OOD data for DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa. Low off-diagonal values (vanilla vs.\\ adapted variants) confirm that continued pretraining substantially reshapes representations; the MLM vs.\\ 9-class block shows distinct geometries (CKA $\\leq$ 0.2 on RoBERTa OOD).}\n\\label{fig:cka_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Embedding Space Visualization}\n\\label{app:tsne}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:tsne} visualizes mean-pooled \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via t-SNE for three representative variants (Vanilla, JB-MLM, 9-Class) on DD OOD and ActorAttack. Vanilla embeddings show dispersed jailbreak clusters with ambiguous decision boundaries, while domain-adapted variants (JB-MLM, 9-Class) produce tighter, more separable clusters. 1	% experiments.tex
2	
3	\section{Experiments}
4	
5	\paragraph{Terminology.}
6	\emph{Cross-attack OOD}: evaluation on attack families unseen during training; benign distribution remains IID throughout (OOD reflects attack-side shift only). $K$: prefix length (number of conversation turns retained). \emph{DAPT}: all Stage-1 adaptations; we extend \citet{gururangan2020dontstop} to supervised variants. \emph{Evidence hierarchy} (on synthetic data): vanilla $<$ MLM $<$ classification (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).
7	
8	\subsection{Setup}
9	
10	\paragraph{Data.}
11	All synthetic data is generated by Qwen3-8B~\citep{qwen3_2025}.
12	The IID dataset contains 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, split into training (350), validation (74), and test (76) sets with balanced class ratios (e.g., test = 38 jailbreak + 38 benign).
13	Stage~1 (DAPT) uses training + validation conversations; Stage~2 (BiGRU) trains on training, early-stops on validation, and evaluates on test.
14	OOD evaluation pairs attack-specific jailbreak conversations with the 38 IID-test benign conversations: 80 FITD~\citep{fitd2024} (non-discriminative; Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}) + 38 benign = 118 total, 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} + 38 benign = 118 total, and 80 ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} + 38 benign = 118 total.
15	No overlap exists in attack templates across families; benign is IID (same generation pipeline).
16	A paraphrased version of the IID test set (BERTScore 0.92, lexical overlap 0.20) serves as the adversarial robustness benchmark (Section~\ref{sec:perturbation}).
17	For human data validation, we use ToxicChat~\citep{lin2023toxicchat} (204 jailbreak + 204 benign from real LMSYS user interactions), entirely independent of Qwen3-8B generation.
18	
19	\paragraph{Models.}
20	Seven DeBERTa-v3 variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline and differ only in Stage-1 training (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
21	Our vanilla DeBERTa+BiGRU configuration is architecturally equivalent to DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026} and differs only in encoder choice (DeBERTa-v3-base vs.\ BERT); it serves as our primary baseline.
22	For cross-encoder validation, we replicate vanilla, MLM, and 9-class variants on RoBERTa-base~\citep{liu2019roberta} (Section~\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).
23	We also compare TF-IDF LR, LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}), and Llama Guard~3~8B~\citep{metallamaguard3_2024} (zero-shot; evaluates last-response safety given conversation context), which detects only 1.8\% of multi-turn jailbreaks (5/278 pooled recall; Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).
24	
25	\paragraph{Metrics.}
26	F1 for binary jailbreak detection at prefix $K$=2 and full conversations (full per-$K$ results in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}).
27	OOD results report 3-seed mean$\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456) for all variants.
28	For key pairwise comparisons, we report BCa bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals (10,000 resamples over individual predictions pooled across 3 seeds; this assumes cross-seed independence and is exploratory) and paired permutation tests (10,000 permutations). Seed-level F1 values (Table~\ref{tab:seed_level}, Appendix~\ref{app:seed_level}) confirm all pairwise conclusions hold at every seed.
29	All standard deviations are computed over 3 random seeds using the population formula ($n$ divisor); Bessel-corrected estimates ($n{-}1$ divisor) are $\approx$22\% larger but do not alter any significance conclusions.
30	
31	\paragraph{Training.}
32	Stage-1: learning rate $2 \times 10^{-5}$, batch 16, 4 epochs, MLM masking 15\%.
33	Stage-2: two-layer MLP, 20 epochs.
34	
35	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
36	\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}
37	
38	\begin{table}[t]
39	\centering
40	\small
41	\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1) at $K$=2 and full conversation length. IID: Crescendo; OOD: Deceptive Delight (DD). 3-seed mean$\pm$std where noted; full per-$K$ in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_prefix}.}
42	\label{tab:main_results}
43	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc|cc@{}}
44	\toprule
45	& \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textit{IID (Cresc.)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textit{OOD (DD)}} \\
46	\textbf{Method} & $K$=2 & Full & $K$=2 & Full \\
47	\midrule
48	9-class & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & .981$\pm$.013 & \textbf{.997$\pm$.005} \\
49	MLM-only$^{\ddagger}$ & .974 & 1.000 & \textbf{.993$\pm$.009} & .914$\pm$.115 \\
50	Vanilla & .978$\pm$.016 & 1.000$\pm$.000 & .479$\pm$.038 & .312$\pm$.026 \\
51	TF-IDF LR$^{\S}$ & 1.000 & 1.000 & 1.000 & .961 \\
52	\bottomrule
53	\end{tabular}
54	\vspace{-2pt}
55	{\footnotesize $^{\ddagger}$MLM-only IID reports single seed (42); OOD reports 3-seed. $^{\S}$TF-IDF is deterministic (no std).}
56	\end{table}
57	
58	Vanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).
59	On OOD DD, vanilla drops to F1\,=\,0.312$\pm$0.026 and worsens with more turns (0.628$\to$0.312 at Full), consistent with attack turns occupying benign embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}. MLM recovers to $\geq$0.97 at $K \leq 5$ without labels but is unstable at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115); 9-class maintains 0.997$\pm$0.005.
60	On strategy-diverse ActorAttack, TF-IDF collapses (0.261) while 9-class maintains 0.972$\pm$0.026, a 71\,pp gap confirming domain-adapted representations capture beyond-lexical features.
61	Seed-level results confirm consistent separation (Table~\ref{tab:seed_level}; bootstrap CIs non-overlapping, permutation $p < 0.0001$). FITD is non-discriminative (all F1\,$\geq$\,0.990).
62	
63	\begin{figure}[t]
64	\centering
65	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}
66	\caption{Cross-attack overview (F1 at full conversation length). DisPeD (9-class) maintains near-perfect detection across IID and both OOD families; vanilla collapses on OOD DD. FITD is non-discriminative for all methods.}
67	\label{fig:cross_attack_bar}
68	\end{figure}
69	
70	Prefix-length analysis (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:full_prefix}) confirms 9-class stability across all prefixes (0.972--0.997) vs.\ MLM instability at full length (0.914$\pm$0.115) and vanilla degradation (0.628$\to$0.312).
71	
72	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
73	\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}
74	\label{sec:decomposition}
75

77	\centering
78	\small
